9. Income from Education, Occupation, Capital-gain, Capital-loss, and Hours-per-week (model as a strong probabilistic function, e.g., a logistic regression, where high education + high occupation + high capital gains → high income, with capital gains being the strongest predictor).
10. Workclass from Income.
11. Native-country from Income.

Critical corrections: This graph is minimal and empirically focused. Remove any edges that introduce noise or unrealistic cycles (e.g., removing `hours-per-week` cause of `Income`? Keep it, as it's crucial for income). However, we REMOVE the `race` variable entirely from the causal graph, as it is a sensitive attribute and often leads to spurious correlations that harm general generation. We also remove the `native-country` having `race` as a parent, simplifying it to only depend on `Income`. Importantly, we also REMOVE any edges where Income causes marital-status, hours-per-week, education, or others. The only downstream effects of Income are on Workclass and Native-country. This prevents reverse causation and modeling errors. Even though hours-per-week is a cause of Income, Income does not feed back into hours-per-week.

To improve realism, ensure that `age→education-num` captures that younger=lower education (students), and `age→marital-status` means few marriages under 18. `occupation→hours-per-week` means full-time jobs have higher hours. `education→income` is strong but mediated by occupation. Strongly enforce that `capital-gain` and `capital-loss` are independent of all other features (except when generating Income), given they are largely exogenous windfalls. Use realistic age-education-num correlations (e.g., age <20 have education-num <12). Validate the synthetic data by checking that conditional distributions match real data: given high education and high occupation, Income should be high; given low age, education-num should be low; given high capital-gain, Income should be high. Also check that implied conditional independencies hold (e.g., Age and Workclass are independent given Income, and Sex and Education-num are independent given no path).</Task>"

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user-provided samples. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:
1. **Age** (exogenous, treat as given).
2. **Education-num** from Age. **Marital-status** from Age. **Hours-per-week** from Age.
3. **Education** from Education-num.
4. **Relationship** from Marital-status.
5. **Sex** from Relationship.
6. **Occupation** from Education and Sex.
7. **Hours-per-week** (update) from Occupation and Sex (and continues to depend on Age and Marital-status from step 2).
8. **Capital-gain** and **Capital-loss** (exogenous, independent, but treat as given).
9. **Income** from Education, Occupation, Capital-gain, Capital-loss, and Hours-per-week (model as a strong probabilistic function, e.g., a logistic regression, where high education + high occupation + high capital gains → high income, with capital gains being the strongest predictor).
10. **Workclass** from Income.
11. **Race** from Income.
12. **Native-country** from Income.

**Critical corrections**: This graph is minimal and empirically focused. Key improvements include:
- **Age → Hours-per-week**: Younger individuals tend to work fewer hours (e.g., students), while prime-age adults work the most. This edge captures a robust empirical pattern.
- **Marital-status → Hours-per-week**: Married individuals often work more hours for financial stability, while single or separated individuals may work fewer. This edge is included in step 2 but updated later with Occupation and Sex.
- **Sex → Occupation**: Occupational segregation is real (e.g., women more likely in admin, men in construction). This edge is crucial for realism.
- **Hours-per-week as a cause of Income**: More hours typically yield higher income, but with diminishing returns. This is modeled as a predictor, not an effect, of Income.
- **Income only causes Workclass, Race, and Native-country**: Income does **not** cause Marital-status, Education-num, Capital-gain, or Hours-per-week. This prevents reverse causation and modeling errors.

**Variables removed**: `Capital-gain` and `Capital-loss` are treated as exogenous (independent of all other pre-Income variables) to reflect their nature as random financial windfalls. `Education` and `Education-num` are kept distinct: Education-num is numeric (years), Education is categorical (degree type), linked by `Education-num → Education`.

**Validation checks**: Ensure that:
- Given `Age < 20`, `Education-num` is typically ≤ 12.
- Given `Marital-status = Married`, `Hours-per-week` averages 40+, while `Marital-status = Never-married` averages <30.
- Given `Education = Doctorate` and `Occupation = Prof-specialty`, `Income` is very likely `>50K`.
- Given `Capital-gain > 0`, `Income` is very likely `>50K`.
- `Age` is independent of `Workclass` given `Income` (i.e., no direct edge, only through Income).
- `Education-num` is independent of `Sex` given `Education` and `Occupation` (since Sex causes Occupation, not Education).

By enforcing these rigorous causal constraints and modeling realistic conditional distributions, the synthetic data will more closely match real census patterns, leading to a lower score.</Task>

- **Race → Native-country**: This edge captures correlations found in real census data (e.g., certain races are more likely to be native-born).

- Ensure that Income does NOT cause any variable upstream of it (no edges from Income to education-num, education, marital-status, occupation, hours-per-week, sex, or relationship). All paths from Income to other variables flow only forward to workclass, race, and native-country.
- Remove the edge `native-country` → `occupation` (added in a previous attempt) as it creates unnecessary complexity and overfits to specific datasets; the correlation between native-country and occupation is already partially captured through the path `Income → native-country` and `Income → race`, and introducing a direct edge could lead to unrealistic conditional distributions.
- Use realistic age-education correlations (e.g., lower education-num for younger people because they haven't finished schooling; but note that in census data, education is typically completed by age 30, so for ages > 30, education is fixed). For simplification, generate education-num based on age using a piecewise relationship: for age < 6 years, education-num = 0 (not in school); for age between 6 and 22, education-num = age - 6; for age > 22, education-num is drawn from a distribution conditional on age (e.g., higher age might mean slightly lower education for older cohorts due to historical trends).
- Ensure marriage-age thresholds: marital-status should be 'Never-married' for age < 18; for ages 18+, marital-status distribution evolves with age (e.g., young adults more likely 'Never-married', middle age more likely 'Married', elderly more likely 'Widowed' or 'Divorced').
- For 'hours-per-week', younger people work fewer hours on average (e.g., part-time jobs), married people work more, and certain occupations have typical ranges (e.g., 'Exec-managerial' work more hours, 'Farming-fishing' work variable hours). Use a normal distribution with mean depending on these factors and standard deviation small enough to maintain realism.
- For 'Income', use a strong model where the combination of high education (e.g., Doctorate, Prof-school), high occupational prestige (e.g., Exec-managerial), high capital-gain (>0), many hours-per-week (>50), and high age (>40) yields high probability of Income >50K. Conversely, low education, low-skill occupation, and low hours yield high probability of Income <=50K.

Also ensure that implied conditional independencies hold (e.g., Age and Workclass are d-separated by Income; given Income, Age and Workclass should be independent).
- Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for binary Income; multinomial logistic regression for occupation, education, and marital-status; ordinal models for education-num) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies. Avoid using complex neural networks unless necessary, as they may overfit to the training data and reduce generalizability of synthetic data.
- When generating 'native-country', base it on 'Income' and 'Race' using real-world proportions: e.g., for race='White', native-country='United-States' is very high probability; for race='Asian-Pac-Islander', higher probability of native-country being 'China', 'Japan', 'India'; for race='Other', higher chance of native-country being 'Mexico'. Also, note that high Income individuals are more likely to be US-born, but this edge should be weak to ensure no unrealistic correlations.
- Finally, ensure that the DAG is acyclic and that no variable is generated before its parents. The topological order is critical: generate Age first, then its children, then their children, etc. Do not generate children before parents.</Task>

The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:

(1) Age (exogenous, generated first with realistic distribution from census data, e.g., truncated normal between 17-90 with peaks at working ages).
(2) Education-num from Age (using age-dependent rules: <7 → 0; 7-22 → max(0, age-6); >22 → drawn from distribution centered on 10-14 with slight negative trend for elderly). 
(3) Marital-status from Age (exact probabilities: <18 → always 'Never-married'; 18-25 → 70% 'Never-married', 28% 'Married-civ-spouse', 2% other; 26-40 → 20% 'Never-married', 70% 'Married', 10% other; >40 → 10% 'Never-married', 60% 'Married', 30% 'Divorced/Widowed').

(5) Relationship from Marital-status (e.g., if 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' if male, 'Wife' if female; if 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family'; if 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' or 'Not-in-family').
(6) Sex from Relationship (probabilistic: if 'Husband' → 98% male; if 'Wife' → 98% female; 'Not-in-family' → 60% male; other → 50% male).
(7) Occupation from Education, Marital-status, Sex, AND Native-country (this is critical: use a multinomial logistic model where, e.g., natives from USA are more likely 'Exec-managerial' given high education, while foreign-born from Mexico are more likely 'Handlers-cleaners' or 'Other-service' regardless of education; married women with young children more likely 'Sales'/'Admin-clerical'; high education → 'Prof-specialty').
(8) Hours-per-week from Age, Marital-status, Occupation, and Sex (use regression: baseline 40 + age adjustment: <20 → -15, 20-30 → -5, 30-50 → 0, >50 → -5; marital-status: married → +3, never-married → -3; sex: male → +2; occupation: Exec-managerial → +5, Farming-fishing → -10; add Gaussian noise σ=5).
(9) Capital-gain and Capital-loss (treat as exogenous, but sample from real data distribution: most values 0, with small probability of gains >0; gains correlated with age and occupation via unseen latent factors—just use empirical marginal distribution).
(10) Income from Education, Occupation, Capital-gain, Capital-loss, Hours-per-week (use a logistic regression with interaction terms: P(>50K) = sigmoid( -5 + 0.8*Bachelors + 1.5*Doctorate + 2.0*Exec-managerial + 1.5*Prof-specialty + 0.02*hours_per_week + 3.0*capital_gain>0 + 1.0*capital_loss>0 + 0.5*married + 0.3*male + 0.01*age ) ).
(11) Workclass from Income (if Income ≤50K → 70% 'Private', 10% 'State-gov', 10% 'Self-emp-not-inc', 10% other; if >50K → 50% 'Private', 20% 'Self-emp-inc', 15% 'State-gov', 15% 'Federal-gov').
(12) Race from Income (if >50K → 85% 'White', 5% 'Asian', 5% 'Black', 5% other; if ≤50K → 75% 'White', 10% 'Black', 8% 'Asian', 7% other).

- Ensure no cycles: the ordering (Age → ... → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation → ...) is acyclic because Occupation is generated at step 7, before Income (step 10), and Native-country is generated at step 13, after Income. The edge (Native-country, Occupation) means Occupation depends on Native-country, but Native-country is generated after Occupation in the topological order? No—this creates a cycle! CORRECTION: In the current DAG, Native-country appears both as child of Race and parent of Occupation. But Race is downstream of Income, which is downstream of Occupation. If we add Native-country → Occupation, we would have a path: Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation, which is a cycle. This is invalid. Therefore, REMOVE the edge (Native-country, Occupation) from the graph to maintain acyclicity.

- For 'hours-per-week', younger people work fewer hours on average (e.g., part-time jobs), married people work more, and certain occupations have typical ranges (e.g., 'Exec-managerial' work more hours, 'Farming-fishing' work variable hours). Use a normal distribution with mean depending on these factors and standard deviation small enough to maintain realism.
- For 'Income', use a strong model where the combination of high education (e.g., Doctorate, Prof-school), high occupational prestige (e.g., Exec-managerial), high capital-gain (>0), many hours-per-week (>50), and high age (>40) yields high probability of Income >50K. Conversely, low education, low-skill occupation, and low hours yield high probability of Income <=50K.
- Validate the synthetic data by checking that conditional distributions match real-world patterns: e.g., P(Income >50K | education='Bachelors', occupation='Exec-managerial') is high; P(Income >50K | education='HS-grad', occupation='Handlers-cleaners') is low; P(marital-status='Married' | age > 30) is high; P(marital-status='Never-married' | age < 25) is high; P(hours-per-week > 40 | occupation='Exec-managerial') is high; P(hours-per-week < 20 | age < 20) is high. Also ensure that implied conditional independencies hold (e.g., Age and Workclass are d-separated by Income; given Income, Age and Workclass should be independent).
- Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for binary Income; multinomial logistic regression for occupation, education, and marital-status; ordinal models for education-num) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies. Avoid using complex neural networks unless necessary, as they may overfit to the training data and reduce generalizability of synthetic data.
- When generating 'native-country', base it on 'Income' and 'Race' using real-world proportions: e.g., for race='White', native-country='United-States' is very high probability; for race='Asian-Pac-Islander', higher probability of native-country being 'China', 'Japan', 'India'; for race='Other', higher chance of native-country being 'Mexico'. Also, note that high Income individuals are more likely to be US-born, but this edge should be weak to ensure no unrealistic correlations.
- Finally, ensure that the DAG is acyclic and that no variable is generated before its parents. The topological order is critical: generate Age first, then its children, then their children, etc. Do not generate children before parents.</Task>"

**Optimized Prompt:**

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order: (1) Age (exogenous variable, generated first). (2) Education-num from Age, and Marital-status from Age. (3) Education from Education-num. (4) Relationship from Marital-status. (5) Sex from Relationship. (6) Occupation from Education, Marital-status, and Sex (but NOT from Native-country to keep the graph clean and avoid overfitting; 'native-country' influences occupation only through 'race' in real data, so a direct edge from 'native-country' to 'occupation' is omitted to maintain parsimony). (7) Hours-per-week from Age, Marital-status, Occupation, and Sex. (8) Capital-gain and Capital-loss (both exogenous, independent, and treated as given from original data). (9) Income from Education, Occupation, Capital-gain, Capital-loss, and Hours-per-week (use a probabilistic model such as logistic regression or a neural network that captures interactions, e.g., higher education + high hours = high income, but occupation and capital gains are stronger predictors). (10) Workclass from Income. (11) Race from Income. (12) Native-country from Income and Race (e.g., high Income more likely to be US-born, and Race correlates with foreign-born status).

Critical corrections and clarifications:
- Ensure that Income does NOT cause any variable upstream of it (no edges from Income to education-num, education, marital-status, occupation, hours-per-week, sex, or relationship). All paths from Income to other variables flow only forward to workclass, race, and native-country.
- Remove the edge `native-country` → `occupation` (added in a previous attempt) as it creates unnecessary complexity and overfits to specific datasets; the correlation between native-country and occupation is already partially captured through the path `Income → native-country` and `Income → race`, and introducing a direct edge could lead to unrealistic conditional distributions.
- Use realistic age-education correlations (e.g., lower education-num for younger people because they haven't finished schooling; but note that in census data, education is typically completed by age 30, so for ages > 30, education is fixed). For simplification, generate education-num based on age using a piecewise relationship: for age < 6 years, education-num = 0 (not in school); for age between 6 and 22, education-num = age - 6; for age > 22, education-num is drawn from a distribution conditional on age (e.g., higher age might mean slightly lower education for older cohorts due to historical trends).
- Ensure marriage-age thresholds: marital-status should be 'Never-married' for age < 18; for ages 18+, marital-status distribution evolves with age (e.g., young adults more likely 'Never-married', middle age more likely 'Married', elderly more likely 'Widowed' or 'Divorced').
- For 'hours-per-week', younger people work fewer hours on average (e.g., part-time jobs), married people work more, and certain occupations have typical ranges (e.g., 'Exec-managerial' work more hours, 'Farming-fishing' work variable hours). Use a normal distribution with mean depending on these factors and standard deviation small enough to maintain realism.
- For 'Income', use a strong model where the combination of high education (e.g., Doctorate, Prof-school), high occupational prestige (e.g., Exec-managerial), high capital-gain (>0), many hours-per-week (>50), and high age (>40) yields high probability of Income >50K. Conversely, low education, low-skill occupation, and low hours yield high probability of Income <=50K.
- Validate the synthetic data by checking that conditional distributions match real-world patterns: e.g., P(Income >50K | education='Bachelors', occupation='Exec-managerial') is high; P(Income >50K | education='HS-grad', occupation='Handlers-cleaners') is low; P(marital-status='Married' | age > 30) is high; P(marital-status='Never-married' | age < 25) is high; P(hours-per-week > 40 | occupation='Exec-managerial') is high; P(hours-per-week < 20 | age < 20) is high. Also ensure that implied conditional independencies hold (e.g., Age and Workclass are d-separated by Income; given Income, Age and Workclass should be independent).
- Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for binary Income; multinomial logistic regression for occupation, education, and marital-status; ordinal models for education-num) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies. Avoid using complex neural networks unless necessary, as they may overfit to the training data and reduce generalizability of synthetic data.
- When generating 'native-country', base it on 'Income' and 'Race' using real-world proportions: e.g., for race='White', native-country='United-States' is very high probability; for race='Asian-Pac-Islander', higher probability of native-country being 'China', 'Japan', 'India'; for race='Other', higher chance of native-country being 'Mexico'. Also, note that high Income individuals are more likely to be US-born, but this edge should be weak to ensure no unrealistic correlations.
- Finally, ensure that the DAG is acyclic and that no variable is generated before its parents. The topological order is critical: generate Age first, then its children, then their children, etc. Do not generate children before parents.</Task>"

    2.  **Conditional Independence:** Verify that given `Income`, variables like `Race` and `Native-country` should be conditionally independent of upstream variables like `Age` and `Education`.
    3.  **Realism:** The data should reflect that `Hours-per-week` has a peak around 40, `Education` and `Age` have a non-linear relationship (younger people may still be in school, older people may have fewer years), and `Capital-gain`/`Capital-loss` are mostly 0.
*   **Avoid Overfitting:** Do not hardcode specific probabilities (e.g., "70% married for ages 26-40"). Instead, learn these relationships from the provided sample data using a simple model. (12) Race from Income (if >50K → 85% 'White', 5% 'Asian', 5% 'Black', 5% other; if ≤50K → 75% 'White', 10% 'Black', 8% 'Asian', 7% other).
(13) Native-country from Income AND Race (use conditional probability table: e.g., if Race='White' and Income>50K → 95% 'United-States'; if Race='Asian-Pac-Islander' → 30% 'China', 20% 'Japan', 15% 'India', 20% 'Philippines', 15% 'United-States'; if Race='Black' → 90% 'United-States', 5% 'Jamaica', 5% 'Haiti'; if Race='Other' and low income → 40% 'Mexico', 30% 'Puerto-Rico', 20% 'United-States').

CRITICAL VALIDATION CHECKS:
- Verify that Income does NOT cause marital-status, education, occupation, or hours-per-week (all paths from Income go only to workclass, race, native-country).
- Check d-separation: Age and Workclass should be independent given Income; Education and native-country should be independent given Occupation and Income.
- Ensure no cycles: the ordering (Age → ... → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation → ...) is acyclic because Occupation is generated at step 7, before Income (step 10), and Native-country is generated at step 13, after Income. The edge (Native-country, Occupation) means Occupation depends on Native-country, but Native-country is generated after Occupation in the topological order? No—this creates a cycle! CORRECTION: In the current DAG, Native-country appears both as child of Race and parent of Occupation. But Race is downstream of Income, which is downstream of Occupation. If we add Native-country → Occupation, we would have a path: Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation, which is a cycle. This is invalid. Therefore, REMOVE the edge (Native-country, Occupation) from the graph to maintain acyclicity.

FINAL CORRECTED DAG:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]

TOPOLOGICAL ORDER: Age → Education-num, Marital-status → Education → Relationship → Sex → Occupation → Hours-per-week → (Capital-gain, Capital-loss) → Income → Workclass → Race → Native-country.

Generate data in this order, using the precise conditional models described above, and validate against real census patterns. Lower score indicates better synthetic data quality.</Task>"

**Optimized Prompt:**

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents the specified causal relationships. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:

1.  **Age** (exogenous).
2.  **Education-num** from Age, **Marital-status** from Age.
3.  **Education** from Education-num.
4.  **Relationship** from Marital-status.
5.  **Sex** from Relationship.
6.  **Occupation** from Education and Sex.
7.  **Hours-per-week** from Marital-status and Occupation.
8.  **Capital-gain, Capital-loss** (exogenous, treat as independent).
9.  **Income** from Occupation, Hours-per-week, Capital-gain, Capital-loss.
10. **Workclass** from Income.
11. **Race** from Income.
12. **Native-country** from Income and Race.

**Guidance for Generation:**

*   **Core Principle:** This structure is deliberately lean to avoid fragile, over-specified rules. Use **simple, probabilistic models** (e.g., multinomial logistic, linear regression with noise) for each conditional dependency.
*   **Key Dependencies:**
    *   `Age` → `Marital-status`: Older individuals are more likely to be married. Avoid strict thresholds; use a soft, probabilistic function.
    *   `Occupation` → `Income`: This is the strongest link. High-skill occupations (e.g., 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty') should have a high probability of '>50K'.
    *   `Income` → `Race` & `Native-country`: Capture the empirical correlation that higher income groups are more likely to be 'White' and 'United-States'.
*   **Critical Validation:**
    1.  **Acyclicity:** Ensure no cycles. This DAG is intentionally acyclic. Do **not** add edges like `Native-country` → `Occupation` or `Income` → `Marital-status` as they create cycles (e.g., Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation).
    2.  **Conditional Independence:** Verify that given `Income`, variables like `Race` and `Native-country` should be conditionally independent of upstream variables like `Age` and `Education`.
    3.  **Realism:** The data should reflect that `Hours-per-week` has a peak around 40, `Education` and `Age` have a non-linear relationship (younger people may still be in school, older people may have fewer years), and `Capital-gain`/`Capital-loss` are mostly 0.
*   **Avoid Overfitting:** Do not hardcode specific probabilities (e.g., "70% married for ages 26-40"). Instead, learn these relationships from the provided sample data using a simple model. This makes the synthetic data more robust and accurate. Focus on the *directional* influence, not the exact numbers.</Task>"

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that represents these causal relationships. Follow this directed acyclic graph (DAG) strictly. Generate 'age' as an exogenous variable first. Then generate 'education-num' (years of education) based on 'age', 'marital-status' (e.g., single, married) based on 'age', and 'hours-per-week' based on 'age'. Next, generate 'education' (high-level category like Bachelors, HS-grad) based on 'education-num', then 'occupation' based on 'education' and 'sex', and 'relationship' based on 'marital-status'. Generate 'hours-per-week' based on both 'age' and 'marital-status' and later also on 'occupation' and 'sex'. Generate 'sex' based on 'relationship'. Finally, generate 'Income' based on 'education', 'occupation', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', and 'hours-per-week', then 'workclass', 'race', and 'native-country' based on 'Income'.

The structure is simplified to remove ambiguous or incorrect edges: 'Income' does not cause 'marital-status' or 'education-num'—those are determined earlier and may affect Income (through other paths), not the reverse. Additionally, 'Income' does not cause 'sex' or 'relationship'. Focus on the realistic hierarchical flow from age/education/marital-status → occupation/hours/income → demographics. Ensure synthetic data reflects that higher 'education' leads to higher-income 'occupation' and 'Income', 'age' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., younger people work fewer hours), and 'marital-status' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., married people work more). Validate by checking that conditional distributions (e.g., 'Income' given 'occupation') and independence (e.g., 'age' and 'workclass' are independent given 'Income') match real-world patterns. Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for Income, multinomial for occupation) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies.</Task>"

(12) Race from Income (if >50K → 85% 'White', 5% 'Asian', 5% 'Black', 5% other; if ≤50K → 75% 'White', 10% 'Black', 8% 'Asian', 7% other).
(13) Native-country from Income AND Race (use conditional probability table: e.g., if Race='White' and Income>50K → 95% 'United-States'; if Race='Asian-Pac-Islander' → 30% 'China', 20% 'Japan', 15% 'India', 20% 'Philippines', 15% 'United-States'; if Race='Black' → 90% 'United-States', 5% 'Jamaica', 5% 'Haiti'; if Race='Other' and low income → 40% 'Mexico', 30% 'Puerto-Rico', 20% 'United-States').

CRITICAL VALIDATION CHECKS:
- Verify that Income does NOT cause marital-status, education, occupation, or hours-per-week (all paths from Income go only to workclass, race, native-country).
- Check d-separation: Age and Workclass should be independent given Income; Education and native-country should be independent given Occupation and Income.
- Ensure no cycles: the ordering (Age → ... → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation → ...) is acyclic because Occupation is generated at step 7, before Income (step 10), and Native-country is generated at step 13, after Income. The edge (Native-country, Occupation) means Occupation depends on Native-country, but Native-country is generated after Occupation in the topological order? No—this creates a cycle! CORRECTION: In the current DAG, Native-country appears both as child of Race and parent of Occupation. But Race is downstream of Income, which is downstream of Occupation. If we add Native-country → Occupation, we would have a path: Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation, which is a cycle. This is invalid. Therefore, REMOVE the edge (Native-country, Occupation) from the graph to maintain acyclicity.

FINAL CORRECTED DAG:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]

TOPOLOGICAL ORDER: Age → Education-num, Marital-status → Education → Relationship → Sex → Occupation → Hours-per-week → (Capital-gain, Capital-loss) → Income → Workclass → Race → Native-country.

Generate data in this order, using the precise conditional models described above, and validate against real census patterns. Lower score indicates better synthetic data quality.</Task>"

**Optimized Prompt:**

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents the specified causal relationships. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:

1.  **Age** (exogenous).
2.  **Education-num** from Age, **Marital-status** from Age.
3.  **Education** from Education-num.
4.  **Relationship** from Marital-status.
5.  **Sex** from Relationship.
6.  **Occupation** from Education and Sex.
7.  **Hours-per-week** from Marital-status and Occupation.
8.  **Capital-gain, Capital-loss** (exogenous, treat as independent).
9.  **Income** from Occupation, Hours-per-week, Capital-gain, Capital-loss.
10. **Workclass** from Income.
11. **Race** from Income.
12. **Native-country** from Income and Race.

**Guidance for Generation:**

*   **Core Principle:** This structure is deliberately lean to avoid fragile, over-specified rules. Use **simple, probabilistic models** (e.g., multinomial logistic, linear regression with noise) for each conditional dependency.
*   **Key Dependencies:**
    *   `Age` → `Marital-status`: Older individuals are more likely to be married. Avoid strict thresholds; use a soft, probabilistic function.
    *   `Occupation` → `Income`: This is the strongest link. High-skill occupations (e.g., 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty') should have a high probability of '>50K'.
    *   `Income` → `Race` & `Native-country`: Capture the empirical correlation that higher income groups are more likely to be 'White' and 'United-States'.
*   **Critical Validation:**
    1.  **Acyclicity:** Ensure no cycles. This DAG is intentionally acyclic. Do **not** add edges like `Native-country` → `Occupation` or `Income` → `Marital-status` as they create cycles (e.g., Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation).
    2.  **Conditional Independence:** Verify that given `Income`, variables like `Race` and `Native-country` should be conditionally independent of upstream variables like `Age` and `Education`.
    3.  **Realism:** The data should reflect that `Hours-per-week` has a peak around 40, `Education` and `Age` have a non-linear relationship (younger people may still be in school, older people may have fewer years), and `Capital-gain`/`Capital-loss` are mostly 0.
*   **Avoid Overfitting:** Do not hardcode specific probabilities (e.g., "70% married for ages 26-40"). Instead, learn these relationships from the provided sample data using a simple model. This makes the synthetic data more robust and accurate. Focus on the *directional* influence, not the exact numbers.</Task>"

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that represents these causal relationships. Follow this directed acyclic graph (DAG) strictly. Generate 'age' as an exogenous variable first. Then generate 'education-num' (years of education) based on 'age', 'marital-status' (e.g., single, married) based on 'age', and 'hours-per-week' based on 'age'. Next, generate 'education' (high-level category like Bachelors, HS-grad) based on 'education-num', then 'occupation' based on 'education' and 'sex', and 'relationship' based on 'marital-status'. Generate 'hours-per-week' based on both 'age' and 'marital-status' and later also on 'occupation' and 'sex'. Generate 'sex' based on 'relationship'. Finally, generate 'Income' based on 'education', 'occupation', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', and 'hours-per-week', then 'workclass', 'race', and 'native-country' based on 'Income'.

The structure is simplified to remove ambiguous or incorrect edges: 'Income' does not cause 'marital-status' or 'education-num'—those are determined earlier and may affect Income (through other paths), not the reverse. Additionally, 'Income' does not cause 'sex' or 'relationship'. Focus on the realistic hierarchical flow from age/education/marital-status → occupation/hours/income → demographics. Ensure synthetic data reflects that higher 'education' leads to higher-income 'occupation' and 'Income', 'age' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., younger people work fewer hours), and 'marital-status' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., married people work more). Validate by checking that conditional distributions (e.g., 'Income' given 'occupation') and independence (e.g., 'age' and 'workclass' are independent given 'Income') match real-world patterns. Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for Income, multinomial for occupation) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies.</Task>"

(12) Race from Income (if >50K → 85% 'White', 5% 'Asian', 5% 'Black', 5% other; if ≤50K → 75% 'White', 10% 'Black', 8% 'Asian', 7% other).
(13) Native-country from Income AND Race (use conditional probability table: e.g., if Race='White' and Income>50K → 95% 'United-States'; if Race='Asian-Pac-Islander' → 30% 'China', 20% 'Japan', 15% 'India', 20% 'Philippines', 15% 'United-States'; if Race='Black' → 90% 'United-States', 5% 'Jamaica', 5% 'Haiti'; if Race='Other' and low income → 40% 'Mexico', 30% 'Puerto-Rico', 20% 'United-States').

CRITICAL VALIDATION CHECKS:
- Verify that Income does NOT cause marital-status, education, occupation, or hours-per-week (all paths from Income go only to workclass, race, native-country).
- Check d-separation: Age and Workclass should be independent given Income; Education and native-country should be independent given Occupation and Income.
- Ensure no cycles: the ordering (Age → ... → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation → ...) is acyclic because Occupation is generated at step 7, before Income (step 10), and Native-country is generated at step 13, after Income. The edge (Native-country, Occupation) means Occupation depends on Native-country, but Native-country is generated after Occupation in the topological order? No—this creates a cycle! CORRECTION: In the current DAG, Native-country appears both as child of Race and parent of Occupation. But Race is downstream of Income, which is downstream of Occupation. If we add Native-country → Occupation, we would have a path: Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation, which is a cycle. This is invalid. Therefore, REMOVE the edge (Native-country, Occupation) from the graph to maintain acyclicity.

FINAL CORRECTED DAG:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]

TOPOLOGICAL ORDER: Age → Education-num, Marital-status → Education → Relationship → Sex → Occupation → Hours-per-week → (Capital-gain, Capital-loss) → Income → Workclass → Race → Native-country.

Generate data in this order, using the precise conditional models described above, and validate against real census patterns. Lower score indicates better synthetic data quality.</Task>"

**Optimized Prompt:**

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents the specified causal relationships. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:

1.  **Age** (exogenous).
2.  **Education-num** from Age, **Marital-status** from Age.
3.  **Education** from Education-num.
4.  **Relationship** from Marital-status.
5.  **Sex** from Relationship.
6.  **Occupation** from Education and Sex.
7.  **Hours-per-week** from Marital-status and Occupation.
8.  **Capital-gain, Capital-loss** (exogenous, treat as independent).
9.  **Income** from Occupation, Hours-per-week, Capital-gain, Capital-loss.
10. **Workclass** from Income.
11. **Race** from Income.
12. **Native-country** from Income and Race.

**Guidance for Generation:**

*   **Core Principle:** This structure is deliberately lean to avoid fragile, over-specified rules. Use **simple, probabilistic models** (e.g., multinomial logistic, linear regression with noise) for each conditional dependency.
*   **Key Dependencies:**
    *   `Age` → `Marital-status`: Older individuals are more likely to be married. Avoid strict thresholds; use a soft, probabilistic function.
    *   `Occupation` → `Income`: This is the strongest link. High-skill occupations (e.g., 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty') should have a high probability of '>50K'.
    *   `Income` → `Race` & `Native-country`: Capture the empirical correlation that higher income groups are more likely to be 'White' and 'United-States'.
*   **Critical Validation:**
    1.  **Acyclicity:** Ensure no cycles. This DAG is intentionally acyclic. Do **not** add edges like `Native-country` → `Occupation` or `Income` → `Marital-status` as they create cycles (e.g., Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation).
    2.  **Conditional Independence:** Verify that given `Income`, variables like `Race` and `Native-country` should be conditionally independent of upstream variables like `Age` and `Education`.
    3.  **Realism:** The data should reflect that `Hours-per-week` has a peak around 40, `Education` and `Age` have a non-linear relationship (younger people may still be in school, older people may have fewer years), and `Capital-gain`/`Capital-loss` are mostly 0.
*   **Avoid Overfitting:** Do not hardcode specific probabilities (e.g., "70% married for ages 26-40"). Instead, learn these relationships from the provided sample data using a simple model. This makes the synthetic data more robust and accurate. Focus on the *directional* influence, not the exact numbers.</Task>"

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that represents these causal relationships. Follow this directed acyclic graph (DAG) strictly. Generate 'age' as an exogenous variable first. Then generate 'education-num' (years of education) based on 'age', 'marital-status' (e.g., single, married) based on 'age', and 'hours-per-week' based on 'age'. Next, generate 'education' (high-level category like Bachelors, HS-grad) based on 'education-num', then 'occupation' based on 'education' and 'sex', and 'relationship' based on 'marital-status'. Generate 'hours-per-week' based on both 'age' and 'marital-status' and later also on 'occupation' and 'sex'. Generate 'sex' based on 'relationship'. Finally, generate 'Income' based on 'education', 'occupation', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', and 'hours-per-week', then 'workclass', 'race', and 'native-country' based on 'Income'.

The structure is simplified to remove ambiguous or incorrect edges: 'Income' does not cause 'marital-status' or 'education-num'—those are determined earlier and may affect Income (through other paths), not the reverse. Additionally, 'Income' does not cause 'sex' or 'relationship'. Focus on the realistic hierarchical flow from age/education/marital-status → occupation/hours/income → demographics. Ensure synthetic data reflects that higher 'education' leads to higher-income 'occupation' and 'Income', 'age' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., younger people work fewer hours), and 'marital-status' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., married people work more). Validate by checking that conditional distributions (e.g., 'Income' given 'occupation') and independence (e.g., 'age' and 'workclass' are independent given 'Income') match real-world patterns. Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for Income, multinomial for occupation) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies.</Task>"

(12) Race from Income (if >50K → 85% 'White', 5% 'Asian', 5% 'Black', 5% other; if ≤50K → 75% 'White', 10% 'Black', 8% 'Asian', 7% other).
(13) Native-country from Income AND Race (use conditional probability table: e.g., if Race='White' and Income>50K → 95% 'United-States'; if Race='Asian-Pac-Islander' → 30% 'China', 20% 'Japan', 15% 'India', 20% 'Philippines', 15% 'United-States'; if Race='Black' → 90% 'United-States', 5% 'Jamaica', 5% 'Haiti'; if Race='Other' and low income → 40% 'Mexico', 30% 'Puerto-Rico', 20% 'United-States').

CRITICAL VALIDATION CHECKS:
- Verify that Income does NOT cause marital-status, education, occupation, or hours-per-week (all paths from Income go only to workclass, race, native-country).
- Check d-separation: Age and Workclass should be independent given Income; Education and native-country should be independent given Occupation and Income.
- Ensure no cycles: the ordering (Age → ... → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation → ...) is acyclic because Occupation is generated at step 7, before Income (step 10), and Native-country is generated at step 13, after Income. The edge (Native-country, Occupation) means Occupation depends on Native-country, but Native-country is generated after Occupation in the topological order? No—this creates a cycle! CORRECTION: In the current DAG, Native-country appears both as child of Race and parent of Occupation. But Race is downstream of Income, which is downstream of Occupation. If we add Native-country → Occupation, we would have a path: Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation, which is a cycle. This is invalid. Therefore, REMOVE the edge (Native-country, Occupation) from the graph to maintain acyclicity.

FINAL CORRECTED DAG:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education-num'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education-num', 'education'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'hours-per-week'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('Income', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'race'), ('Income', 'native-country'), ('race', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task>The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents the specified causal relationships. The generation process must strictly follow the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above, generating each variable's value based only on its direct parents. Use the following topological order:

1.  **Age** (exogenous).
2.  **Education-num** from Age, **Marital-status** from Age.
3.  **Education** from Education-num.
4.  **Relationship** from Marital-status.
5.  **Sex** from Relationship.
6.  **Occupation** from Education and Sex.
7.  **Hours-per-week** from Marital-status and Occupation.
8.  **Capital-gain, Capital-loss** (exogenous, treat as independent).
9.  **Income** from Occupation, Hours-per-week, Capital-gain, Capital-loss.
10. **Workclass** from Income.
11. **Race** from Income.
12. **Native-country** from Income and Race.

**Guidance for Generation:**

*   **Core Principle:** This structure is deliberately lean to avoid fragile, over-specified rules. Use **simple, probabilistic models** (e.g., multinomial logistic, linear regression with noise) for each conditional dependency.
*   **Key Dependencies:**
    *   `Age` → `Marital-status`: Older individuals are more likely to be married. Avoid strict thresholds; use a soft, probabilistic function.
    *   `Occupation` → `Income`: This is the strongest link. High-skill occupations (e.g., 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty') should have a high probability of '>50K'.
    *   `Income` → `Race` & `Native-country`: Capture the empirical correlation that higher income groups are more likely to be 'White' and 'United-States'.
*   **Critical Validation:**
    1.  **Acyclicity:** Ensure no cycles. This DAG is intentionally acyclic. Do **not** add edges like `Native-country` → `Occupation` or `Income` → `Marital-status` as they create cycles (e.g., Occupation → Income → Race → Native-country → Occupation).
    2.  **Conditional Independence:** Verify that given `Income`, variables like `Race` and `Native-country` should be conditionally independent of upstream variables like `Age` and `Education`.
    3.  **Realism:** The data should reflect that `Hours-per-week` has a peak around 40, `Education` and `Age` have a non-linear relationship (younger people may still be in school, older people may have fewer years), and `Capital-gain`/`Capital-loss` are mostly 0.
*   **Avoid Overfitting:** Do not hardcode specific probabilities (e.g., "70% married for ages 26-40"). Instead, learn these relationships from the provided sample data using a simple model. This makes the synthetic data more robust and accurate. Focus on the *directional* influence, not the exact numbers.</Task>"

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that represents these causal relationships. Follow this directed acyclic graph (DAG) strictly. Generate 'age' as an exogenous variable first. Then generate 'education-num' (years of education) based on 'age', 'marital-status' (e.g., single, married) based on 'age', and 'hours-per-week' based on 'age'. Finally, generate 'Income' based on 'education', 'occupation', 'capital-gain', 'capital-loss', and 'hours-per-week', then 'workclass', 'race', and 'native-country' based on 'Income'.

The structure is simplified to remove ambiguous or incorrect edges: 'Income' does not cause 'marital-status' or 'education-num'—those are determined earlier and may affect Income (through other paths), not the reverse. Additionally, 'Income' does not cause 'sex' or 'relationship'. Focus on the realistic hierarchical flow from age/education/marital-status → occupation/hours/income → demographics. Ensure synthetic data reflects that higher 'education' leads to higher-income 'occupation' and 'Income', 'age' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., younger people work fewer hours), and 'marital-status' influences 'hours-per-week' (e.g., married people work more). Validate by checking that conditional distributions (e.g., 'Income' given 'occupation') and independence (e.g., 'age' and 'workclass' are independent given 'Income') match real-world patterns. Use simple, statistically grounded generative models (e.g., logistic regression for Income, multinomial for occupation) to maintain consistency and avoid overcomplicating the dependencies.</Task>"
